Prop 3: Mia ran to the left after dodging the attack.
Prop 4: The door is to the right of Mia when she ran.

**Step 3: If applicable propositions exist, state which and explain**

This directly contradicts White's statement that she ran *to* the door after running to the left. If the door is to her right, and she ran to the left, she wouldn't be running *towards* the door. This creates a spatial contradiction.

LLM OUTPUT (RAW RESPONSE):
------------------------------
**Step 1: Analyze the spatial contradiction**

The contradiction lies between Testimony 4 ("...tucked safely away in her left breast pocket") and Evidence 6 ("Magenta Flower...torn from the victim's clothes"). Testimony 4 states Ms. Crane placed the letter in her *left breast pocket*. However, Evidence 6 describes a magenta flower (corsage) being *torn from the victim's clothes*. If the letter was in her left breast pocket, it's unlikely a flower would be *torn* from that same location. The presence of the flower suggests it was on her chest, potentially obscuring or displacing the letter.

**Step 2: Check if any spatial propositions apply**

Prop 1: "The letter from Kay was inside Ms. Crane's left breast pocket" directly supports Testimony 4. However, the presence of the torn flower contradicts this.

**Step 3: If applicable propositions exist, state which and explain**

Prop 1 directly contradicts the implication of Evidence 6. Prop 1 states the letter was *inside* the left breast pocket. Evidence 6 implies something else (the flower) was present and removed from the same general area, making it unlikely the letter remained undisturbed in the pocket. The spatial relationship described in Prop 1 is incompatible with the spatial reality suggested by Evidence 6.

**Step 4: If none of the listed propositions apply, explain**

Not applicable, as Prop 1 directly addresses the spatial relationship in question.

**Step 2:** **Check if any spatial propositions** from the list above apply to this contradiction

**Step 3:** **If applicable propositions exist,** explicitly state which proposition(s) and explain how they reveal the contradiction

**Step 4:** **If none of the listed propositions apply,** explain the spatial reasoning used to identify the contradiction

**The answer JSON should strictly be the last line, with nothing before or after it.**

LLM OUTPUT (RAW RESPONSE):
------------------------------
**Step 1: Analyze the spatial contradiction**

Kay's testimony (Testimony 3) states she saw something red "from under the cherry tree." However, Kay's testimony (Testimony 4) states someone was walking towards her "from the other side of that stand" (referring to the concession stand). This creates a spatial contradiction: if she was *under* the cherry tree, how could she see someone approaching from the *other side* of the concession stand? The concession stand is positioned to the right of the cherry tree (Prop 1). To see someone on the other side of the concession stand while being under the cherry tree would require an impossible vantage point.

**Step 2: Check if any spatial propositions apply**

Prop 1: The concession stand is to the right of the cherry tree on the Bigg Building viewing platform. This proposition directly relates to the spatial contradiction.

**Step 3: If applicable propositions exist, state which and explain**

Prop 1 establishes the relative positions of the cherry tree and the concession stand. If Kay was *under* the cherry tree, her view would be obstructed by the tree itself, and the concession stand would be to her right, making it difficult, if not impossible, to see someone approaching from the "other side" of the concession stand. The contradiction arises because Kay's description of her vantage point and the location of the person approaching are incompatible given the established spatial relationship.

**Step 4: If none of the listed propositions apply, explain**

N/A - Prop 1 directly applies.

**Step 5: Provide your answer in JSON format**

LLM OUTPUT (RAW RESPONSE):
------------------------------
**Step 1: Analyze the spatial contradiction**

The contradiction lies between Testimony 4 ("There's dirt inside the costume. It must have gotten there when the body was hidden inside.") and Evidence 5 ("The body lying face-up on top of the dirt, and yet there's no dirt on the front of the body."). Testimony 4 implies that if Shaun was inside the Taurusaurus costume and hid the body, dirt would be inside the costume. However, Evidence 5 states that the body was found on top of dirt, but the *front* of the body was clean of dirt. This suggests the body wasn't simply hidden *inside* the costume, as that would likely have resulted in dirt on the front of the body as well.

**Step 2: Check if any spatial propositions apply**

Prop 1: Shaun was inside the Taurusaurus costume and near to the body of President Wang. This proposition is directly relevant as it establishes Shaun's location relative to the body.
Prop 5: The monster footprints are near to the location where Excelsius Winner dug up the ransom money. This is less directly relevant but could be connected if the body was moved after the initial discovery.

**Step 3: If applicable propositions exist, state which and explain**

Prop 1 directly highlights the contradiction. The testimony suggests dirt *should* be inside the costume if the body was hidden there. However, the evidence indicates a lack of dirt on the body's front, implying a different scenario than simply placing the body inside the costume. The spatial relationship described in Prop 1 (Shaun in the costume near the body) is challenged by the lack of dirt on the body, suggesting the body was placed *on* the dirt, not *in* the costume.

**Step 4: If none of the listed propositions apply, explain**

Not applicable, as Prop 1 directly addresses the spatial contradiction.

**Step 3: If applicable propositions exist**

Prop 3 directly reveals the contradiction. Ini states she didn't see anyone on the way to the Channeling Chamber. However, Prop 3 states the Sacred Urn was in the hallway before being moved to the Channeling Chamber. The route from the Side Room to the Channeling Chamber passes through the hallway (as per Ini's statement and the Guidemap). Prop 5: Kay Faraday was near the concession stand and then at the Bigg Building viewing platform - This is not directly relevant to the contradiction.

If the red raincoat was in the storeroom, it *must* have been heading towards the viewing platform. **Step 2: Check if any spatial propositions** from the list above apply to this contradiction

**Step 4: If none of the listed propositions apply,** explain the spatial reasoning used to identify the contradiction

The spatial reasoning involves understanding the *temporal* relationship between the two events (strangulation and stabbing) as described by the autopsy report and the testimony. The autopsy report establishes a clear sequence: strangulation *then* stabbing. Testimony 5, by implying premeditation focused on the stabbing, suggests the stabbing was the primary and initial act. The spatial element is the location of the knife *within* the victim's body, but the contradiction is about the *timing* of its use relative to the strangulation.

**Step 3:** **If applicable propositions exist,** explicitly state which proposition(s) and explain how they reveal the contradiction

**Step 4:** **If none of the listed propositions apply,** explain the spatial reasoning used to identify the contradiction

**Step 5:** **Provide your answer in JSON format**

**Your answer must end with a JSON format like so:** {"evidence": 2, "testimony": 3}

**The answer JSON should strictly be the last line, with nothing before or after it.**

LLM OUTPUT (RAW RESPONSE):
------------------------------
**Step 1: Analyze the spatial contradiction**

The contradiction lies between Evidence 21 (Committee Chamber Blood) and Testimony 4 (Franziska's statement about the bloodstain being in the meeting room). Franziska states the bloodstain *proves* the crime occurred in the P.I.C. meeting room. However, Evidence 21 indicates blood was found in the Committee Chamber, suggesting a potential discrepancy in the location of the crime or an attempt to mislead investigators.

**Step 2: Check if any spatial propositions apply**

Prop 1: The storeroom is above the P.I.C. meeting room. - This doesn't directly address the contradiction.
Prop 2: The Committee chamber is disconnected from the storeroom. - This is relevant. If the Committee chamber is disconnected from the storeroom, it suggests a separate area, and the presence of blood there is more suspicious.
Prop 3: The victim was in the P.I.C. meeting room and the culprit was also in the P.I.C. meeting room. - This directly contradicts the presence of blood in the Committee Chamber.
Prop 4: The bloodstain in the Committee chamber is near the keycard found in the victim's hand - This reinforces the contradiction, linking the Committee Chamber to the victim.
Prop 5: The secret elevator is between the P.I.C. meeting room and the storeroom. - This doesn't directly address the contradiction.

**Step 3: Propositions revealing the contradiction**

Propositions 2 and 3 are most relevant. Prop 3 states both victim and culprit were in the P.I.C. meeting room, implying the crime occurred there. Prop 2 states the Committee chamber is disconnected from the storeroom, suggesting it's a separate location. The presence of blood in the Committee Chamber (Evidence 21) directly challenges Prop 3, suggesting the crime may have involved movement between locations, or that Franziska's assertion about the crime scene is incorrect. Prop 4 further strengthens this by linking the bloodstain to the victim's keycard.

**Step 4: Spatial reasoning**

The spatial reasoning highlights a conflict in the established crime scene. Franziska insists the P.I.C. meeting room is the sole crime scene. However, the discovery of blood in the Committee Chamber, a spatially separate location, casts doubt on this assertion. The disconnected nature of the Committee chamber (Prop 2) makes the presence of blood there even more suspicious, suggesting a deliberate attempt to obscure the true location of the crime or a movement of the victim/culprit between rooms.

**Step 5:** {"evidence": 21, "testimony": 4}

  "testimony": 4

**Step 1: Analyze the spatial contradiction**

This implies a spatial relationship where the bust and the window are positioned in a way that prevents simultaneous observation and lifting.

**Step 2: Check if any spatial propositions apply**

Prop 1: Acro is inside the lodging house and the window is outside the lodging house.
Prop 2: The bust is above Acro and Acro is below the bust.
Prop 5: The bust is disconnected from the window.

All three propositions are relevant. Prop 1 establishes the fundamental spatial separation – Acro is *inside* and the window is *outside*. Prop 2 describes a vertical relationship between Acro and the bust. Prop 5 reinforces the idea that the bust and window are not directly connected, further supporting the difficulty of observing through the window while lifting the bust.

**Step 3: Explain how propositions reveal the contradiction**

Prop 1 is the most crucial. If Acro is *inside* the lodging house and the window is *outside*, it inherently limits his ability to see out of the window while performing an action *inside* the lodging house, such as lifting a heavy object like the bust. Prop 2 adds to this by suggesting the bust is above him, making it even more difficult to look out the window while lifting. Prop 5 simply confirms the spatial separation. Acro's testimony (3 & 4) stating he couldn't see the victim's head due to this spatial arrangement directly aligns with these propositions.

Butz's account places him arriving at the bridge shortly *after* the lightning strike.

**Step 2: Check if any spatial propositions** from the list above apply to this contradiction

Prop 4: Dusky Bridge is to the right of the shack where Butz was staying.
Prop 5: The crystal sphere is near to Dusky Bridge.

**Step 3: If applicable propositions exist,** explicitly state which proposition(s) and explain how they reveal the contradiction

Prop 4 is relevant. Butz's testimony places him traveling from his shack (below Eagle Bridge) to Dusky Bridge. The contradiction arises because the timing of his arrival at Dusky Bridge, as described in his testimony, doesn't align with the timing of the lightning strike at Dusky Bridge (Evidence 7). If he left immediately after hearing the thunder, he would have arrived *before* the lightning, not shortly after. This suggests his account of the timing is inaccurate, and potentially his location at the time of the lightning strike. Prop 5 is less directly relevant, but reinforces the spatial connection between Dusky Bridge and the events Butz describes.

**Step 4: If none of the listed propositions apply,** explain the spatial reasoning used to identify the contradiction

The spatial reasoning involves understanding the relative locations (shack, Eagle Bridge, Dusky Bridge) and the temporal sequence of events (thunder, lightning strike, Butz's arrival at Dusky Bridge). The contradiction highlights an inconsistency in Butz's timeline, suggesting he may be misrepresenting his movements or the timing of events.

The photo, however, shows the victim *without* a scarf, and near the broken end of the bridge. The wet scarf found on the bridge (Evidence 5) suggests a later event, contradicting the photo's depiction and the note's instructions.

**Step 4: If none of the listed propositions apply,** explain the spatial reasoning used to identify the contradiction

**The answer JSON should strictly be the last line, with nothing before or after it.**

LLM OUTPUT (RAW RESPONSE):
------------------------------
**Step 1: Analyze the spatial contradiction**

The contradiction lies between Evidence 8 (Wright's Hand) and Evidence 9 (Victim's Hand), and Testimony 3 (Olga). Evidence 8 and 9 state that both the defendant (Wright) and the victim (Smith) had a full house. Testimony 3, however, states that Smith's hand had three aces and Wright's had two, implying a total of five aces – which is impossible in a standard poker hand and contradicts the claim of both having full houses. The spatial element here is the *arrangement* of the cards in each hand.

**Step 2: Check if any spatial propositions apply**

Prop 2: "The defendant's hand, containing a full house, is near the victim's hand, also containing a full house" directly applies to this contradiction.

**Step 3: Proposition application**

Prop 2 states that the hands are "near" each other. This implies a spatial proximity. However, the contradiction arises because the *contents* of those hands, as described by Olga, are incompatible with the claim of both being full houses. The proximity (as stated in Prop 2) highlights the absurdity of the situation: two hands, physically close, supposedly containing full houses, but with card arrangements that make this impossible. Olga's testimony directly challenges the validity of the hands being full houses, thus contradicting the spatial proposition's implied validity.

**Step 4: Spatial reasoning**

The contradiction isn't about the physical location of the hands relative to the table or other objects. It's about the spatial arrangement *within* each hand. The arrangement of cards within each hand is what creates the contradiction. If both hands were truly full houses, the card arrangements would be consistent with that definition. Olga's testimony reveals that the card arrangements are inconsistent, making the claim of both hands being full houses false.

LLM OUTPUT (RAW RESPONSE):

**Step 1: Analyze the spatial contradiction**

The contradiction lies between Testimony 1 ("That's when I saw Juniper dragging a large mat from behind the stage.") and Evidence 18 ("There is a tall construction screen between the stage and the vantage point near the maintenance area. Two banners are lying on the stage."). O'Conner states he saw Juniper dragging a mat *from behind* the stage. However, Evidence 18 describes a construction screen *between* the stage and O'Conner's vantage point, and banners lying *on* the stage. This implies that if O'Conner was observing from his vantage point, he would have had a clear view of the stage and anything behind it, including Juniper dragging a mat. The presence of the screen obstructs this view.

**Step 2: Check if any spatial propositions apply**

Prop 2: The stage is behind the construction screen.
Prop 3: O'Conner was near the stage and the maintenance area.

**Step 3: If applicable propositions exist, state which and explain**

Both Prop 2 and Prop 3 are relevant. Prop 2 establishes the spatial relationship between the stage and the construction screen – the stage is *behind* the screen. Prop 3 places O'Conner in a location *near* the stage. Combining these, if O'Conner was indeed near the stage (as per Prop 3), and the stage is behind a construction screen (as per Prop 2), it becomes difficult to believe he could clearly see Juniper dragging a mat *from behind* the stage, as stated in Testimony 1. The screen would obstruct his view.

N/A - The listed propositions directly address the spatial relationships involved in the contradiction.

This contradicts the implied spatial relationship described by Andrews. If she knocked the vase over, it would have fallen *onto* the guitar case, but the photo shows them already side-by-side, suggesting the vase was already broken.

This contradicts the implied spatial relationship between L'Belle and the demon, given the sequence of events. The testimonies suggest a linear progression of events in the hallway, and Prop 1 locks L'Belle's position relative to the phone, creating a spatial inconsistency with the demon's emergence.

**The answer JSON should strictly be the last line, with nothing before or after it.**

LLM OUTPUT (RAW RESPONSE):
------------------------------
**Step 1: Analyze the spatial contradiction**

The contradiction lies between Testimony 0 and Testimony 3, both provided by Fairplay and Furst respectively. Fairplay (Testimony 0 & 2) states he distinctly remembers seeing blood on *both* hands of the assailant. Furst (Testimony 3) claims he didn't see anything until he heard a scream. This creates a spatial contradiction because if Furst didn't see anything until *after* the scream, he couldn't have observed the assailant's hands, let alone confirm blood on both. Fairplay's observation implies the assailant was visible and active *before* the scream, while Furst's testimony suggests he was unaware of any activity until *after* the scream.

**Step 2: Check if any spatial propositions apply**

Prop 2: Beppo was inside the omnibus and Fairplay was on the roof deck of the omnibus. This proposition is relevant. It establishes the spatial relationship between Fairplay and the scene of the crime. Fairplay was positioned on the roof deck, while Furst was also on the roof deck. This difference in vantage point could explain why Furst didn't see anything, but it doesn't resolve the core contradiction about the assailant's hands.

Prop 4: The bloodstain on the defendant's glove was near to the bloodstain on the omnibus frame. This is not directly relevant to the contradiction between Fairplay and Furst's testimonies.

**Step 3: If applicable propositions exist, explicitly state which proposition(s) and explain how they reveal the contradiction**

Prop 2 is relevant. Fairplay's position on the roof deck provided a potentially clearer view of the interior of the omnibus than Furst's. This difference in spatial perspective could account for Fairplay's observation of the assailant's bloodied hands, while Furst, from his position, didn't witness the event. However, it doesn't negate the core contradiction: Fairplay saw blood on both hands, while Furst saw nothing until after the scream.

**Step 4: If none of the listed propositions apply, explain the spatial reasoning used to identify the contradiction**

The spatial reasoning is based on the concept of observation and vantage point. Fairplay's testimony implies a direct visual observation of the assailant's hands *before* the scream. Furst's testimony explicitly denies any visual observation until *after* the scream. The spatial contradiction arises from the incompatibility of these two accounts regarding the timing and visibility of the assailant's actions.

Nash's statement, however, suggests Windibank was still alive and potentially confronting someone *before* the yell, which contradicts Ringo's account of fleeing *after* the gunshot. The proposition itself doesn't resolve the contradiction; it merely underscores the conflicting narratives surrounding the victim's location.

**Step 4: If none of the listed propositions apply, explain the spatial reasoning used to identify the contradiction**

The spatial reasoning focuses on the implied actions and sequence of events. Testimony 3 describes an action (hiding the body) that suggests the body was placed in the trunk *after* death. Evidence 4 presents a static scene (the photo) where the body is already in the trunk, raising questions about the order of events and whether the scene was manipulated. The contradiction isn't about *where* the body was, but *how* it got there, and the spatial context of the photo challenges the narrative of active concealment presented in the testimony.

This creates a spatial conflict: the defendant can't be standing *in front* of someone who is *inside* a lantern.

**Step 2: Check if any spatial propositions apply**

**Step 3: If applicable propositions exist, explicitly state which proposition(s) and explain how they reveal the contradiction**

However, Evidence 6 states the victim was face up *inside* the broken lantern. This means the Time Keeper (and therefore the defendant, according to Testimony 2) would have to be in front of the lantern, not directly in front of the victim *inside* the lantern. The contradiction arises because Testimony 2 implies a direct, unobstructed view of the victim, while Evidence 6 describes a spatial barrier (the lantern) between the defendant and the victim.
